<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
OK so the layout is:
- Page 5: end of §3.5, §4 Experiments starts
- Page 6: Table 1 at top, then §4.1 Per-Example Divergence text (confidence margin, routing, multiplicity variation)
- Page 7: Table 2 at top, then multiplicity text continuation, Figure 2 (task_specificity), §4.2 Decoupling text
- Page 8: continuation of §4.2, §4.3 Robustness, §5 Conclusion, Limitations, Code and Data, start of References
- Page 9: Figure 3 (jaccard heatmap) at top, References continue


The issue is that the full-width figure is appearing too late because in two-column mode, `figure*[t]` can only float to the top of the next page. Since §4.3 is on page 8, the figure ends up on page 9. To move it earlier, I need to place the figure definition on page 7, but that page is already packed with Table 2 at the top and Figure 2 in the middle.

If I move the figure* definition to page 7 (after the multiplicity variation section), LaTeX would place it at the top of page 8. The question is whether everything else fits below it—the continuation of §4.2, all of §4.3, §5, Limitations, Code and Data, and the start of References. I'm calculating whether the remaining 2/3 of page 8 can accommodate roughly 7-8 lines from §4.2 and 2-3 lines from §4.3, plus the other sections.

```text
60	\par\vspace{1pt}
61	{\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}.}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
65	
66	The per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows monotonic convergence ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.
67	
68	\begin{figure}[t]
69	\centering
70	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
71	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
72	\label{fig:task_specificity}
73	\end{figure}
74	
75	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
76	
77	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) with no further increase---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
78	
79	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA: on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
80	
81	Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Within-architecture Jaccard (${\sim}0.33$; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}) is $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$); Pythia seed calibration shows a similar $3{-}4\times$ gap (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
82	
83	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
84	
85	Cross-scale analysis confirms scale-robustness: pairwise Jaccard is stable ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales), though $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp). At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$), suggesting that the \emph{pattern} of multiplicity reflects structural differences while absolute magnitude is modulated by capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
86	
87	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
88	
89	\begin{figure*}[t]
90	\centering
91	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
92	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1). Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$.}
93	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
94	\end{figure*}
95	
96	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves decoupling ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Five-architecture validation (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairs and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
97	
```

This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here."

```text
60	\par\vspace{1pt}
61	{\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}.}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	\begin{figure*}[t]
65	\centering
66	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
67	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1). Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$.}
68	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
69	\end{figure*}
70	
71	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
72	
73	The per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows monotonic convergence ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
78	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
79	\label{fig:task_specificity}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
83	
84	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) with no further increase---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
85	
86	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA: on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
87	
88	Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Within-architecture Jaccard (${\sim}0.33$; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}) is $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$); Pythia seed calibration shows a similar $3{-}4\times$ gap (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
89	
```

Within-architecture Jaccard (${\sim}0.33$; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}) is $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$); Pythia seed calibration shows a similar $3{-}4\times$ gap (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
82	
83	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
```

      "content": "\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nThe per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows monotonic convergence ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\nEven on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\\bar{J} \\approx 0.095$) with no further increase---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nThis saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA: on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq 0.333$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.\n\nBootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}).   "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] R16 审稿报告修复——结构性叙事重组 + 多项 caveat 修复。这次不用验证引用。\n\n## 背景\nR16 审稿报告 Overall 3.0 (Findings)。核心问题：convergence 叙事过度突出（复发 ×3 轮，hedging 无效），routing 在 Conclusion/Abstract 定位过高。以下修复按优先级排列。\n\n## 修复清单\n\n### P1: 结构性叙事重组（R16-N1 + R5-W2 复发）\n这是最重要的修改。R15 的 hedging 修复被 R16 再次 flag，说明\"加更多 caveat\"这条路走不通。需要结构性调整：\n\n**1a. Introduction (introduction.tex)**\n- 找到第一段中 \"cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79%\" 及随后的 BCa CI 数字。**不要删除这些数字**，而是把它们移到 §4.2 (experiments.tex) 的开头段落（作为 aggregate trend 的定量描述）\n- Introduction 中原位替换为定性描述，如 \"aggregate cross-architecture variance decreases on some benchmarks as attention ratio increases\"——不给具体数字，不给读者\"这是主要发现\"的暗示\n- BCa CI [-1.0, -0.7] 在 Intro 中如果保留，必须加 \"(heuristic; n = 3)\" 标注\n\n**1b. §4.2 标题 (experiments.tex)**\n- 将 \\subsection{Decoupling} 改为 \\subsection{Aggregate Trends and Per-Example Independence} 或类似表述\n- \"Decoupling\" 这个标题预设了两个对等现象的对比，给 convergence 不应有的结构性地位。新标题应凸显 per-example independence 为主、aggregate trend 为辅\n\n**1c. [Director 指令] [Patch] R16 审稿报告修复——结构性叙事重组 + 多项 caveat 修复。这次不用验证引用。

## 背景
R16 审稿报告 Overall 3.0 (Findings)。核心问题：convergence 叙事过度突出（复发 ×3 轮，hedging 无效），routing 在 Conclusion/Abstract 定位过高。以下修复按优先级排列。

## 修复清单

### P1: 结构性叙事重组（R16-N1 + R5-W2 复发）
这是最重要的修改。R15 的 hedging 修复被 R16 再次 flag，说明"加更多 caveat"这条路走不通。需要结构性调整：

**1a. Introduction (introduction.tex)**
- 找到第一段中 "cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79%" 及随后的 BCa CI 数字。**不要删除这些数字**，而是把它们移到 §4.2 (experiments.tex) 的开头段落（作为 aggregate trend 的定量描述）
- Introduction 中原位替换为定性描述，如 "aggregate cross-architecture variance decreases on some benchmarks as attention ratio increases"——不给具体数字，不给读者"这是主要发现"的暗示
- BCa CI [-1.0, -0.7] 在 Intro 中如果保留，必须加 "(heuristic; n = 3)" 标注

**1b. §4.2 标题 (experiments.tex)**
- 将 \subsection{Decoupling} 改为 \subsection{Aggregate Trends and Per-Example Independence} 或类似表述
- "Decoupling" 这个标题预设了两个对等现象的对比，给 convergence 不应有的结构性地位。新标题应凸显 per-example independence 为主、aggregate trend 为辅

**1c. Figure 1 caption**
- Figure 1 的 convergence panel (左面板/a 面板) caption 中加上 Bonferroni-adjusted p-value："(Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; non-significant)"
- 确保读者在看到 convergence 趋势时立即知道其统计地位

### P2: Routing 全文一致性降级（R16-W1 + R15-W2）
- **Conclusion (conclusion.tex)**: 找到 routing 作为三大 practitioner implications 之一的段落。将 routing 从 implication 降级为 future direction。当前 "rescue-threshold routing is viable" 类表述改为 "rescue-threshold routing shows preliminary promise on high-α tasks but requires further validation"
- **Abstract**: 检查 Abstract 尾句。如果 routing 仍作为收束句，弱化措辞。当前 "suggesting these differences are partially actionable" 如果已经够弱可以保留
- **§4.1 routing 段落**: 预留位置给 Main Agent 计算的 SD/CI 数据。在 "+5.3pp" 后加占位符 "(SD = X.X; 95\% CI: [X.X, X.X])"，等 Agent 提供数据后填入

### P3: Empirical contribution framing 强化（R16-N3）
- Introduction 中找到 contribution 列表或 empirical novelty 定位段落
- 强化"first systematic characterization"定位，突出两个超出 "known framework in new domain" 的发现：(1) Jaccard saturation at 4% attention (2) 5-architecture 40-pair validation with all p < .01
- **不要**包装为 methodological contribution——R2 会立刻识别为 overselling

### P4: 小修复（批量完成）

**4a. §4.3 (experiments.tex)**: "Five-architecture validation (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms..." → "Five-architecture validation at 340M (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms..."，同行加一句 "Three-architecture results are stable across both 340M and 1.3B scales (Jaccard ≈ 0.08–0.10), providing indirect support for scale robustness."

**4b. Limitations**: 加 1-2 句 scale limitation："Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity."

**4c. §3.4 (method.tex)**: binarization 讨论加 1 句："Conversely, on K-choice tasks, binarization may overestimate hurt-set agreement when two architectures both transition from correct to incorrect but select different wrong answers."

**4d. Abstract**: "+5.3 pp" 后加 "(vs.\ majority vote)" 注明基线

**4e. §3.2 (method.tex)**: taxonomy 描述加 "(informal; not pre-registered; see Appendix~\ref{app:confirmatory})"

**4f. references.bib**: haghighat 条目 venue 更新为 AAAI 2026

**4g. Figure 4 caption (experiments.tex)**: "Mean pairwise $\bar{J}$" → "Mean pairwise $\bar{J}$ (averaged over helped and hurt sets)"

## 约束
- 保持主文 ≤ 8 页
- 不要过度 hedge——当前统计语言已经足够谨慎，问题在于结构权重而非措辞力度
- 修改后 compile 检查无 critical warnings

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}

18	\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}
19	
20	\author{Anonymous}
21	
22	\begin{document}
23	\maketitle
24	
25	\begin{abstract}
26	Practitioners select among hybrid linear recurrent architectures using aggregate benchmarks, but these scores can mask per-example consequences. Across three controlled designs, adding attention affects substantially different examples per architecture (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline); validation across five architectures and ten pairwise comparisons confirms this on all four benchmarks (all $p < .01$). Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, confirming that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden per-example consequences. Confidence-informed routing extracts 36\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA ($+5.3$\,pp), suggesting these differences are partially actionable.
27	\end{abstract}
28	
29	\input{sections/introduction}
30	
31	\input{sections/related_work}

33	\input{sections/method}
34

39	\section*{Limitations}
40	
41	\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run, so we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects. The $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\times$ higher; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and the consistency of significant Jaccard across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$ is 0.017, so LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ does not reach conventional significance.
42	
43	\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open.
44	
45	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation.
46	
47	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
48	
49	All models are from the publicly available model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis code and per-example evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.
50	
51	\bibliography{references}
52	
53	\appendix
54	\input{sections/appendix}
55	
56	\end{document}

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Hybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect largely non-overlapping sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, BCa CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.
4	
5	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
6	
7	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether the phenomenon extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
8	
9	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\% to 25\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.
10	
11	\begin{figure}[t]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}
14	\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}
15	\label{fig:decoupling}
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	Our analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).
19	
20	Aggregate convergence is task-specific and exploratory: of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}).
21	
22	Our contributions are as follows:
23	\begin{itemize}
24	    \item We show that architectural variation under matched training conditions is associated with structured per-example predictive multiplicity ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Extended validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\leq 0.14$ across four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).
25	    \item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\%) across all four benchmarks and shows no further increase at higher ratios, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). This immediate saturation indicates that minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.
26	\end{itemize}
27

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
4	
5	\begin{table*}[t]
6	\centering
7	\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\sigma$ values reported $\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$. \textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\rho = -1.0$. HellaSwag evaluated using normalized accuracy (acc\_norm), the standard metric for this benchmark.}
8	\label{tab:convergence}
9	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
10	\toprule
11	Benchmark & Category & $\rho$ & $p$ & $\sigma_{\text{pure}}$ & $\sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\
12	\midrule
13	\textbf{LAMBADA$^{\ddagger}$} & \textbf{Retrieval} & $\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \textbf{.017} & \textbf{2.41} & \textbf{0.49} & \textbf{4.87} \\
14	\quad\emph{(excl.\ GDN)} & & $-0.7$ & .233 & --- & --- & --- \\
15	BoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\
16	ARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\
17	HellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.4$ & .517 & 1.17 & 1.22 & 0.96 \\
18	PIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\
19	WinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\
20	ARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\
21	MMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\
22	SciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\
23	XStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\
24	\midrule
25	COPA$^{\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\
26	\bottomrule
27	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.} \\
28	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$GDN h3-1 uses 16 attention heads; all other configurations use 32 (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}).} \\
29	\multicolumn{7}{@{}p{\textwidth}@{}}{\footnotesize Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .017 \times 10 = .170$ (all-10-benchmark correction). \textbf{Power is limited: only $|\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ at $n = 5$.}}
30	\end{tabular}
31	\end{table*}
32	
33	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
34	
35	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude (a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01), so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
36	
37	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes. GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
38	
39	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\% accuracy versus 50.9\% for majority vote (+5.3\,pp), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are marginal (+1.0\,pp); on HellaSwag and PIQA, routing provides no improvement. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but fails when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).
42	
43	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.
44	
45	\begin{table}[t]
46	\centering
47	\small
48	\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\rho_\alpha$: Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and $\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}
49	\label{tab:multiplicity}
50	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}
51	\toprule
52	 & $N$ & $\alpha$ & $\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\rho_\alpha$ \\
53	\midrule
54	PIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\
55	HellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\
56	ARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\
57	LAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\
58	\bottomrule
59	\end{tabular}
60	\par\vspace{1pt}
61	{\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}.}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
65	
66	The per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows monotonic convergence ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.
67	
68	\begin{figure}[t]
69	\centering
70	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
71	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
72	\label{fig:task_specificity}
73	\end{figure}
74	
75	\begin{figure*}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
78	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
79	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
80	\end{figure*}
81	
82	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
83	
84	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) with no further increase---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
85	
86	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_saturation}): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}
91	\caption{Cross-benchmark Jaccard saturation. Mean pairwise $\bar{J}$ remains flat across attention ratios on all four benchmarks (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq .333$), confirming that per-example divergence saturates immediately regardless of task type.}
92	\label{fig:jaccard_saturation}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration produces a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jaccard_hierarchy.pdf}
100	\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) is $2\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}
101	\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}
102	\end{figure}
103	
104	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
105	
106	Cross-scale analysis confirms scale-robustness: pairwise Jaccard is stable ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales), though $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp). At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$), suggesting that the \emph{pattern} of multiplicity reflects structural differences while absolute magnitude is modulated by capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
107	
108	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
109	
110	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves decoupling ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Five-architecture validation (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairs and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
111

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) even as aggregate metrics converge on LAMBADA (dependent on GDN inclusion; \S\ref{ssec:robustness}); five-architecture validation confirms this across four benchmarks (all $J \leq 0.14$, $p < .01$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	For practitioners, three implications follow from the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap: (1) $\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution, as multiplicity at this level means $>$20\% of examples change outcome; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity---majority vote fails despite 15--30\% disagreement, but oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped gain; (3) rescue-threshold routing is viable when $\alpha$ is high and disruption thresholds differ across architectures, extracting 36\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA ($+5.3$\,pp; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Deployment-ready routing remains open.
6	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VV5FYYKwyaHe9jQXJhY7nh`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
6	
7	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\% (pure recurrent), 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, and 25\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\%, h12-1 to 7.7\%, and h24-1 to 4\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.
8	
9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
15	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
16	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) is supplementary; its small sample renders $\alpha$ unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).
17	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	All evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.
21	
22	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
23	
24	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
25	
26	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
27	
28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms that results are robust under continuous Jaccard similarity, mitigating concerns about information loss from binarization.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed. We use exact permutation tests for Spearman $\rho$ ($5! = 120$ permutations). With $n = 5$, only $|\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$); all Spearman-based inferences are therefore descriptive and exploratory. We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\% BCa confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}).
45	
```

We classify each example into one of three categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.\n    \\item \\textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.\n    \\item \\textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe net effect for each architecture is $|\\text{helped}| - |\\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.\n\nTo measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \\cap B| / |A \\cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures. Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard} confirms that results are robust under continuous Jaccard similarity, mitigating concerns about information loss from binarization.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Methods}\\label{ssec:statistical_methods}\n\nAll statistical tests are two-tailed. We use exact permutation tests for Spearman $\\rho$ ($5! = 120$ permutations). With $n = 5$, only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ ($p = .017$); all Spearman-based inferences are therefore descriptive and exploratory. We apply Bonferroni correction across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$). Bootstrap 95\\% BCa confidence intervals use 10{,}000 resamples; with $n = 3$ architectures, these are heuristic indicators of effect direction. Within-architecture comparisons use Mann-Whitney U; cross-architecture comparisons use Kruskal-Wallis. Attention weight analysis extracts per-layer cosine similarity and Shannon entropy via forward hooks (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}).\n",

    }

```text
12	\end{itemize}
13	
14	\paragraph{Confirmatory benchmark set.} Six benchmarks were designated as confirmatory for Bonferroni correction: LAMBADA, HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, and MMLU. These were selected as representative of retrieval, reasoning, and mixed categories before any convergence analysis was performed. The taxonomy and benchmark selection were fixed before analysis as an informal pre-analysis plan; we describe this as a confirmatory/exploratory split rather than a formal pre-registration, since no external timestamp exists. The main text applies the more conservative all-10-benchmark correction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .017 \times 10 = .170$). \textbf{Sensitivity:} correcting across only the six confirmatory benchmarks yields adjusted $p = .017 \times 6 = .100$, still above conventional significance.
15	
16	\paragraph{One-sided test.} If one adopts the prior that attention should reduce cross-architecture variance, the one-sided permutation $p$-value for LAMBADA is $1/120 \approx 0.0083$. Under 10-benchmark Bonferroni correction, this yields an adjusted $p = 0.083$, above the conventional threshold. We report two-sided tests throughout the main text as the more conservative choice; the one-sided result is noted here for completeness.
```

1	% Appendix sections for the paper.
2	
3	\section{Confirmatory and Exploratory Analyses}\label{app:benchmark_details}
4	
5	The task taxonomy used in \S\ref{ssec:task_taxonomy} was defined prior to running any convergence analyses. Each benchmark was assigned to exactly one of four categories based on the primary cognitive demand of the task:
6	
7	\begin{itemize}
8	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: tasks requiring prediction from long-range context without multi-step reasoning (LAMBADA).
9	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: reading comprehension with explicit passage support (BoolQ).
10	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: tasks requiring commonsense inference or multi-step reasoning (HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, PIQA).
11	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: tasks combining retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands (ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, XStoryCloze).
12	\end{itemize}
13	
14	\paragraph{Confirmatory benchmark set.} Six benchmarks were designated as confirmatory for Bonferroni correction: LAMBADA, HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, and MMLU. These were selected as representative of retrieval, reasoning, and mixed categories before any convergence analysis was performed. The taxonomy and benchmark selection were fixed before analysis as an informal pre-analysis plan; we describe this as a confirmatory/exploratory split rather than a formal pre-registration, since no external timestamp exists. The main text applies the more conservative all-10-benchmark correction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .017 \times 10 = .170$). \textbf{Sensitivity:} correcting across only the six confirmatory benchmarks yields adjusted $p = .017 \times 6 = .100$, still above conventional significance.
15	
```

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{caption}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nPractitioners select among hybrid linear recurrent architectures using aggregate benchmarks, but these scores can mask per-example consequences. Across three controlled designs, adding attention affects substantially different examples per architecture (Jaccard $\\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline); validation across five architectures and ten pairwise comparisons confirms this on all four benchmarks (all $p < .01$). Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention dose. Majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, confirming that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden per-example consequences. The $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and the consistency of significant Jaccard across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone.     "oldString": "This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, BCa CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs.",
    "newString": "This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: aggregate cross-architecture variance decreases on some benchmarks as attention ratio increases, but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect largely non-overlapping sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, BCa CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether the phenomenon extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAggregate convergence is task-specific and exploratory: of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation under matched training conditions is associated with structured per-example predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Extended validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ across four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks and shows no further increase at higher ratios, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). This immediate saturation indicates that minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "d29eb5a9-ff59-437a-844c-76309d8924bd",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "49035fc3-bdd1-4679-9e35-d6523ff7e356",

  "uuid": "66fd258d-8256-49e8-a16e-81ae4930a283",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "(a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed).",
    "newString": "(a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect largely non-overlapping sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: aggregate cross-architecture variance decreases on some benchmarks as attention ratio increases, but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether the phenomenon extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAggregate convergence is task-specific and exploratory: of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation under matched training conditions is associated with structured per-example predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Extended validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ across four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks and shows no further increase at higher ratios, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). This immediate saturation indicates that minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

(b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}",
